Introduction Pesticides are widely used to control pests on crops effectively. The use of pesticides has resulted in increased productivity of cultivated crops. Thus, pesticides have become an economically essential agricultural resource for modern agriculture [1–3]. Despite the necessity for pesticides, the risks related to pesticide use and their residues in humans and the environment are being reported [4,5]. Therefore, considering the risks to consumers, the environment, and the productivity of crops, agricultural chemical management safety policies are being implemented nationwide. Pesticide residues in domestically distributed and imported

- [page 1] resource for modern agriculture [1–3]. Despite the necessity for pesticides, the risks related to pesticide use and their residues in humans and the environment are being reported [4,5]. Therefore, considering the risks to consumers, the environment, and the productivity of crops, agricultural chemical management safety policies are being implemented nationwide. Pesticide residues in domestically distributed and imported agri-foods are being monitored in an effort to decrease the marketing and consumption of crops that exceed the maximum residue limit (MRL). Moreover, the Min- istry of Food and Drug Safety has established and applied the pre-harvest residue limit (PHRL) in the production stage to systematize the management of pesticide residues before shipment and on a broade

- [page 1] gri-foods are being monitored in an effort to decrease the marketing and consumption of crops that exceed the maximum residue limit (MRL). Moreover, the Min- istry of Food and Drug Safety has established and applied the pre-harvest residue limit (PHRL) in the production stage to systematize the management of pesticide residues before shipment and on a broader scale, to minimize damage to producers and consumers due to the distribution of substandard crops. According to the recommendations on the safe use of pesticides, PHRL is calculated and established using the biological half-life and decay constant, which are calculated each day before shipment after spraying the chem- ical [6]. Therefore, to provide safe food to consumers by preventing substandard crops from exceeding pes

- [page 1] hich are calculated each day before shipment after spraying the chem- ical [6]. Therefore, to provide safe food to consumers by preventing substandard crops from exceeding pesticide residue limits at the domestic distribution stage and to produce agricultural products meeting the pesticide residue tolerance limit of the exporting country, the establishment and management of PHRL is crucial [6]. - [page 3] Summary Pinoxaden was approved on 1 July 2016 by means of Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2016/370 in the framework of Regulation (EC) No 1107/2009 as amended by Commission Implementing Regulations (EU) No 540/2011 and 541/2011. As the active substance was approved after the entry into force of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005 on 2 September 2008, the European Food Safety Authority (EFSA) is required to provide a reasoned opinion on the review of the existing maximum residue levels (MRLs) for that active substance in compliance

- [page 3] inion, which was circulated to Member States and the EURLs for consultation via a written procedure. Comments received by 27 January 2021 were considered during the ﬁnalisation of this reasoned opinion. The following conclusions are derived. The metabolism of pinoxaden in plants was investigated in primary and rotational crops and the processing is not expected to modify the nature of residues. According to the results of the metabolism studies, the residue de ﬁnition for risk assessment can be proposed as sum of M4 and M6 (both free and conjugated), expressed as pinoxaden. For enforcement, in view of the available information on residue trials and analytical methods, two residue de ﬁnitions are presented for further consideration by risk managers: sum of M4 and M6 (both free an

- [page 3] d rotational crops and the processing is not expected to modify the nature of residues. According to the results of the metabolism studies, the residue de ﬁnition for risk assessment can be proposed as sum of M4 and M6 (both free and conjugated), expressed as pinoxaden. For enforcement, in view of the available information on residue trials and analytical methods, two residue de ﬁnitions are presented for further consideration by risk managers: sum of M4 and M6 (both free and conjugated), expressed as pinoxaden (option 1); and sum of M4 and M6 (both free only), expressed as pinoxaden (option 2). These residue de ﬁnitions are restricted to cereals only. A suf ﬁciently validated single-residue method is available for the enforcement of the proposed residue de ﬁnition option 1 at t

These residue de ﬁnitions are restricted to cereals only. A suf ﬁciently validated single-residue method is available for the enforcement of the proposed residue de ﬁnition option 1 at the combined limit of quantiﬁcation (LOQ) of 0.03 mg/kg in dry commodities and 0.05 mg/kg in high water content commodities. - [page 1] KGROUND: Pinoxaden is a new cereal herbicide that provides outstanding levels of post-emergence activity against a broad spectrum of grass weed species for worldwide selective use in both wheat and barley. RESULTS: Factors inﬂuencing activity and tolerance to pinoxaden were in part linked to distinct structural parts of the active ingredient. Three complementary contributions that decisively impact upon the herbicidal potency against grasses were identiﬁed: a preferred 2,6-diethyl-4-methyl aromatic substitution pattern, a dione area suitable for proherbicide formation and beneﬁcial adjuvant effects. The uptake and translocation pattern of pinoxaden when coapplied with its tailored adjuvant were analysed by autoradiography, indicating extensive and rapid penetration, followed by

- [page 1] isively impact upon the herbicidal potency against grasses were identiﬁed: a preferred 2,6-diethyl-4-methyl aromatic substitution pattern, a dione area suitable for proherbicide formation and beneﬁcial adjuvant effects. The uptake and translocation pattern of pinoxaden when coapplied with its tailored adjuvant were analysed by autoradiography, indicating extensive and rapid penetration, followed by effective distribution throughout the plant. Crop injury reduction on incorporation of the [1,4,5]oxadiazepane ring into the aryldione template was reinforced with safener technology. Comparative studies on the behaviour of pinoxaden applied either alone or in combination with the safener cloquintocet-mexyl demonstrated that addition of the safener resulted in signiﬁcant enhancement o

- [page 1] al adjuvant effects. The uptake and translocation pattern of pinoxaden when coapplied with its tailored adjuvant were analysed by autoradiography, indicating extensive and rapid penetration, followed by effective distribution throughout the plant. Crop injury reduction on incorporation of the [1,4,5]oxadiazepane ring into the aryldione template was reinforced with safener technology. Comparative studies on the behaviour of pinoxaden applied either alone or in combination with the safener cloquintocet-mexyl demonstrated that addition of the safener resulted in signiﬁcant enhancement of metabolic degradation in wheat and barley, providing excellent crop tolerance and a substantial selectivity margin without adverse effects on weed control. CONCLUSION: The biological potential of p

- [page 1] and rapid penetration, followed by effective distribution throughout the plant. Crop injury reduction on incorporation of the [1,4,5]oxadiazepane ring into the aryldione template was reinforced with safener technology. Comparative studies on the behaviour of pinoxaden applied either alone or in combination with the safener cloquintocet-mexyl demonstrated that addition of the safener resulted in signiﬁcant enhancement of metabolic degradation in wheat and barley, providing excellent crop tolerance and a substantial selectivity margin without adverse effects on weed control. CONCLUSION: The biological potential of pinoxaden and its active principle pinoxaden dione in terms of grass weed control and tolerance in cereals was fully exploited by inclusion of the safener cloquintocet-

- [page 1] t addition of the safener resulted in signiﬁcant enhancement of metabolic degradation in wheat and barley, providing excellent crop tolerance and a substantial selectivity margin without adverse effects on weed control. - [page 1] 1 INTRODUCTION The major food crops, such as maize, rice and cereals, are attractive to R&D-based crop protection industries owing to their high market volume globally. However, beyond proﬁtability, an increasing world population requires a more efﬁcient crop production output per surface area. Securing crop yield and quality without impacting upon health and environment still remains a challenge. Among other harmful factors, an effective small grain production needs to cope with the high impact of weed infestations. Weeds compete with small grains for space and the natural resources water, nutrients and light. The loss potential due to weeds in worldwide wheat and barley production was estimated to be about 23%, while weed control measures (based on mechanical, biological and

- [page 1] ty, an increasing world population requires a more efﬁcient crop production output per surface area. Securing crop yield and quality without impacting upon health and environment still remains a challenge. Among other harmful factors, an effective small grain production needs to cope with the high impact of weed infestations. Weeds compete with small grains for space and the natural resources water, nutrients and light. The loss potential due to weeds in worldwide wheat and barley production was estimated to be about 23%, while weed control measures (based on mechanical, biological and chemical practices) showed an average crop protection efﬁcacy of 61–65% in these two major crops. 1 Modern small grain production will rely on integrated ∗ Correspondence to: Michel Muehlebach,

The method showed excellent linearities ( R2 = 0.999) at 0.005 − 5 mg/L. - [page 1] mation ABSTRACT: An eﬀective and sensitive method for the determination of isopyrazam (IZM) isomers ( syn-IZM and anti-IZM) and their metabolites ( syn545364 and syn545449) in tomato and soil by ultraperformance liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry (UPLC-MS/MS) was developed in the present study. The method showed excellent linearities ( R2 = 0.999) at 0.005 − 5 mg/L. The recoveries were 92.0 −107%, and the relative standard deviation (RSD) values were lower than 9.40% in tomato and soil matrices at 0.01, 0.1, and 10 mg/kg. The limits of detection (LODs) of the four compounds ranged from 6.88 × 10−5 to 2.70 × 10−4 mg/kg, while the limits of quanti ﬁcation (LOQs) ranged from 2.20 × 10−4 to 9.20 × 10−4 mg/kg. The storage stability test results showed that syn-IZM, a

- [page 1] IZM and anti-IZM in tomato ranged from <0.0100 −0.490 to <0.0100 −0.0850 mg/kg. The present results showed that anti-IZM degraded faster than syn-IZM in tomato and soil, and had a lower residue level in tomato. - [page 1] IZM in tomato ranged from <0.0100 −0.490 to <0.0100 −0.0850 mg/kg. - [page 1] reported that IZM could be a long-lasting residual compound in typical soil, with a persistent DT50 of 244 days, and degraded very slowly in soils under aerobic laboratory conditions, with the DT 50 ranging from 40 to 976 days. 12,13 The two main degradation products of IZM, syn545364 and syn545449, may pose a higher risk than their parent compound to the environment. 5 It was reported that the metabolites of IZM have higher toxicity than their parent compound to organisms. 14 Thus, IZM and its metabolites pose potential threats to the ecological balance and environmental security, which necessitates the research on their dissipation behavior and risk assessments. Currently, only a few studies have developed detection methods for IZM, and liquid chromatography-tandem mass spe

- [page 2] ormation is available from previous papers on the isomer-selective degradation behavior of IZM in the environment, which may be due to the lack of eﬃ cient determination methods. Additionally, it is worth mentioning that the study of the retention behavior of metabolites is also important for risk assessment, but there have been few studies thus far. Hence, in this study, an e ﬃ cient and quick method for the simultaneous determination of isomers ( syn-IZM and anti- IZM) and metabolites ( syn545364 and syn545449) of IZM was established using a modi ﬁed QuEChERS method and ultraperformance liquid chromatography-tandem mass spec- trometry (UPLC-MS/MS). Furthermore, the ﬁeld dissipation trends and terminal residues of the two isomers in soil and tomato were investigated. - [page 3] t was investigated in primary and rotational crops. According to the results of the metabolism studies, the residue de ﬁnition for enforcement can be proposed as isopyrazam (sum of isomers) and for risk assessment as sum of isopyrazam (sum of isomers) and its metabolite CSCD459488 (free and conjugated), expressed as isopyrazam. These residue de ﬁnitions are also applicable to processed commodities. Fully validated analytical methods are available for the enforcement of the proposed residue deﬁnition at the limit of quanti ﬁcation (LOQ) of 0.01 mg/kg in high water content, high oil content and dry matrices. According to the EURLs, isopyrazam can be monitored in high water content, high acid content, dry and high fat content commodities with an LOQ of 0.01 mg/kg. Available residue

- [page 3] te covering the maximum dietary burdens calculated in this review. According to the results of these studies, the residue de ﬁnition for enforcement was proposed as isopyrazam (sum of isomers) and for risk assessment as isopyrazam (sum of isomers) and all its metabolites containing the CSAA798670 moiety, expressed as isopyrazam. An analytical method for the enforcement of the proposed residue de ﬁnition at the LOQ of 0.005 mg/kg (0.0025 mg/kg anti- and syn-isomers) in all matrices is available. According to the EURLs, the LOQ of 0.01 mg/kg may be achievable based on screening data and the general analytical behaviour of isopyrazam in routine analyses. Biodegradation is one of the significant pathways dealing with the high gen- eration of fungicide residues in the environment [2,3]. The expulsion of pesticides and their transformative products is investigated for effective administration and management of the contaminated agrarian zones. - [page 2] e, adsorption, hydrolysis, and photocatalysis are the techniques that have been already evaluated [ 4,5]. - [page 1] 29:2702–2712. # 2010 SETAC Keywords—Degradation Microcosm Macrophytes Algae Photolysis Regulatory risk assessments of crop protection products (CPPs) in surface water are typically based on a range of laboratory studies in which the key processes of sorption, hydrolysis, photolysis, and microbial degradation are evaluated separately in simple, standardized systems, usually conforming to appropriate regional regulatory guidelines [1–5]. These studies provide information on the intrinsic properties of the pesticide in water and in soil or sediment. Hydrolysis studies are conducted under sterile conditions in the dark, to exclude potential microbial and photolytic degradation, respectively. Photolysis studies are also conducted under sterile conditions and at a pH at which hydrolysi

- [page 1] bial degradation are evaluated separately in simple, standardized systems, usually conforming to appropriate regional regulatory guidelines [1–5]. These studies provide information on the intrinsic properties of the pesticide in water and in soil or sediment. Hydrolysis studies are conducted under sterile conditions in the dark, to exclude potential microbial and photolytic degradation, respectively. Photolysis studies are also conducted under sterile conditions and at a pH at which hydrolysis is known to be minimal. The most complex study in the regulatory scheme is conducted in laboratory systems containing water and sediment in small vessels that are incubated in the dark under either aerobic or anaerobic conditions. Potential loss mechanisms in these stud- ies include sorptio

- [page 1] water and in soil or sediment. Hydrolysis studies are conducted under sterile conditions in the dark, to exclude potential microbial and photolytic degradation, respectively. Photolysis studies are also conducted under sterile conditions and at a pH at which hydrolysis is known to be minimal. The most complex study in the regulatory scheme is conducted in laboratory systems containing water and sediment in small vessels that are incubated in the dark under either aerobic or anaerobic conditions. Potential loss mechanisms in these stud- ies include sorption to the sediment surface, hydrolysis, and microbial degradation, because sterile conditions are not used. However, because these studies are conducted in the dark, the contributions of photolysis and metabolism by phototrophic

- [page 1] latory scheme is conducted in laboratory systems containing water and sediment in small vessels that are incubated in the dark under either aerobic or anaerobic conditions. Potential loss mechanisms in these stud- ies include sorption to the sediment surface, hydrolysis, and microbial degradation, because sterile conditions are not used. However, because these studies are conducted in the dark, the contributions of photolysis and metabolism by phototrophic organisms are excluded. Furthermore, no study in the regulatory suite integrates all of these potential processes. Assessing the relative contributions of each of these processes to the fate of a CPP in a natural water body under actual environmental con- ditions is difﬁcult. A better assessment of the environmental fate of the

- [page 1] pharmaceutical compounds [13–15], and muni- tions [16]. The purpose of the current study was to assess the potential signiﬁcance of these degradation processes in the aquatic fate of the novel fungicide isopyrazam. Initially the basic degradative processes of hydrolysis at a range of pH values, direct photol- ysis, and microbial degradation in water–sediment systems were investigated. This was followed by an assessment of degradation in more complex laboratory studies, which inves- tigated the role of additional degradative processes such as indirect photolysis and metabolism by algae and aquatic Environmental Toxicology and Chemistry, Vol. 29, No. 12, pp. 2702–2712, 2010 # 2010 SETAC Printed in the USA DOI: 10.1002/etc.337 * To whom correspondence may be addressed (laurence.hand

- [page 1] H ARRIET J. MORELAND Syngenta, Product Safety Department, Jealott ’s Hill International Research Centre, Bracknell, Berkshire, United Kingdom (Submitted 5 July 2013; Returned for Revision 19 September 2013; Accepted 24 November 2013) Abstract: A surface water mineralization study (according to the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development [OECD] guideline OECD 309) is a new requirement in European Union agrochemical regulations; therefore, industry has little experience with this test. The guideline allows for a number of options within the test design, notably the options to conduct the study under diffuse light and to include an inoculum of suspended sediment. The present study was designed to investigate the potential impact of these options on the degradation rate of

- [page 1] OECD 309 Euphotic zone INTRODUCTION With the implementation of the new directive for the regulation of agrochemicals (European Parliament 1107/2009 [1]), a number of new data requirements have been introduced. In the area of environmental fate, surface water mineralization studies according to Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development [OECD] guideline 309 [2] will be mandatory in new submissions for approval. The study is required unless the applicant can demonstrate contamination of open water bodies (freshwater, estuarine, or marine) cannot occur. As such, the intended protection goal appears to be different from those normally considered in agrochemical regulation, namely ditches, streams, and ponds. There are a number of study designs that can be used to fulﬁll this

- [page 1] ght on the degradation rate of isopyrazam under conditions relevant to large, open water bodies. When assessing the impact of light in degradation studies, it is important that the light intensity used is environmentally relevant; in the case of surface water mineralization studies, the intensity used should be relevant to large, open water bodies. A literature review of light penetration in lakes, estuaries, and oceans was therefore conducted [7 –21]. In all studies reviewed, the key parameter used to describe light penetration was the euphotic zone. This was de ﬁned as the zone in which there is sufﬁcient light penetration for photosynthesis to occur [7,8]. The lower limit of this zone (the euphotic depth) is generally de ﬁned as the depth at which the light intensity is attenuated

- [page 2] context. The secondary aim was to highlight any experimental challenges that may inform on the most appropriate design for implementation of this new data requirement in future submissions. MATERIALS AND METHODS Study design The primary goal of surface water mineralization studies is to quantify the extent of mineralization of the [ 14C]-labeled test compound in microbially active natural surface water. In addition, the extent of primary metabolism should also be investigated. The test compound is applied to the surface water and incubated, with continuous agitation, at constant tempera- ture for up to 60 d. Two test concentrations are required, namely 1 mg/L to 10 mg/L and 10 mg/L to 100 mg/L (with a difference of at least 5 /C2 ). To establish whether degradation is by biotic or a

- [page 2] erimental challenges that may inform on the most appropriate design for implementation of this new data requirement in future submissions. MATERIALS AND METHODS Study design The primary goal of surface water mineralization studies is to quantify the extent of mineralization of the [ 14C]-labeled test compound in microbially active natural surface water. In addition, the extent of primary metabolism should also be investigated. The test compound is applied to the surface water and incubated, with continuous agitation, at constant tempera- ture for up to 60 d. Two test concentrations are required, namely 1 mg/L to 10 mg/L and 10 mg/L to 100 mg/L (with a difference of at least 5 /C2 ). To establish whether degradation is by biotic or abiotic processes, sterile controls are included. A r

Exhaust gases were passed through a 2 M sodium hydroxide solution to trap any [ 14C]-CO2 evolved from mineralization of the test compound. The present study was divided into a number of distinct experiments designed to investigate different variables. These experiments are listed below. Experiment 1 was an investigation of the degradation rate of aniline in pelagic water at 2 different treatment concentrations, designed to demonstrate that the test system was microbially viable and capable of degrading labile compounds. This experiment was conducted under 2 sets of incubation conditions: 1) continuous darkness, and 2) a ﬂuoresce

- [page 3] , and 28 –29 d, duplicate 3 mL aliquots were removed from each biometer ﬂask for analysis. Each sample was then analyzed in duplicate for aniline concentration. The high treatment rate samples (20 mg/L) were analyzed by high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) with ultraviolet detection at 254 nm. The decline in peak area with time was monitored to determine the degradation rate. The low-treatment-rate samples (20 mg/L) were analyzed by liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry by monitoring for the mass transition of 93.9 mass-to-charge ratio (m/z) to 77.1 m/z. Again, the decline in peak area with time was monitored to determine the degradation rate. In each case, the degradation was estimated using single ﬁrst-order (SFO) kinetics using computer-assisted kinetic

All ﬂasks were then incubated on the Sanyo M9536 bench at 20 8C, programmed to give 16 h light and 8 h darkness per day. After an equilibration period of 7 d, the ﬂasks were treated with a [ 14C]-labeled isopyrazam solution in acetonitrile at a concentration of 94 mg/L (a ﬁnal cosolvent concentration of <0.05%). - [page 3] etermine the mass balance) by multiplying the radioactivity per mL in each aliquot by the initial total volume of the sample (thereby accounting for the effect of sequential removal of multiple aliquots for analysis). Each aliquot was analyzed in duplicate by HPLC to determine the levels of isopyrazam present at each time point. The radioactivity in each sodium hydroxide trapping solution was quanti ﬁed by LSC after removal of the ﬁnal aliquots. At the end of the incubation period, each biometer ﬂask was washed with 20 mL acetonitrile and the washings were radio- assayed by LSC and analyzed by HPLC. - [page 1] tract Biodegradation of miticide propargite was carried out in vitro by selected Pseudomonas strains isolated from tea rhizosphere. A total number of 13 strains were isolated and further screened based on their tol- erance level to different concentrations of propargite. Five best strains were selected and further tested for their nutritional requirements. Among the different carbon sources tested glucose exhibited the high- est growth promoting capacity and among nitrogen sources ammonium nitrate supported the growth to the maximum. The ﬁve selected Pseudomonas strain exhibited a range of degradation capabilities. Min- eral salts medium (MSM) amended with glucose provided better environment for degradation with the highest degradation potential in strain SPR 13 followed by SPR 8

- [page 1] sulﬁte) was studied. Propargite is widely applied for controlling a variety of phytophagous mites on many crops includ- ing cotton, vines, fruit trees, vegetables, hops and nuts, etc. [10]. In tea plantations, it is used in controlling red spider mites [11]. Propargite is an irritant in humans and may also be a sensitizer, as several outbreaks of dermatitis was observed in ﬁeld workers exposed to this compound [12] and WHO has classiﬁed propargite as “slightly hazardous” [13]. Propargite is a high priority pesticide for evaluation as a toxic air contaminant, in a ranking in which it tied with cyanazine for ﬁrst place [14]. It is highly toxic to ﬁsh and crustaceans [15]. - [page 1] c. [10]. In tea plantations, it is used in controlling red spider mites [11]. Propargite is an irritant in humans and may also be a sensitizer, as several outbreaks of dermatitis was observed in ﬁeld workers exposed to this compound [12] and WHO has classiﬁed propargite as “slightly hazardous” [13]. Propargite is a high priority pesticide for evaluation as a toxic air contaminant, in a ranking in which it tied with cyanazine for ﬁrst place [14]. It is highly toxic to ﬁsh and crustaceans [15]. Propargite is moderately persistent and immobile in soils. Propargite is a high priority pesticide for evaluation as a toxic air contaminant, in a ranking in which it tied with cyanazine for ﬁrst place [14]. It is highly toxic to ﬁsh and crustaceans [15]. - [page 1] and WHO has classiﬁed propargite as “slightly hazardous” [13]. Propargite is a high priority pesticide for evaluation as a toxic air contaminant, in a ranking in which it tied with cyanazine for ﬁrst place [14]. It is highly toxic to ﬁsh and crustaceans [15]. Propargite is moderately persistent and immobile in soils. In water, it hydrolyzes rapidly (half-life 2.2 days) under alkaline conditions (pH 9), where as moderately persistent at pH 7 (half-life 75 days), and very persistent at pH 5 (half-life 120 days). As tea soil is acidic (4.8–5), the persistence of the chemical is of great concern. So in the present study, an attempt was made to exam- ine the degradation of this chemical by the tea soil microﬂora. For degradation studies, Pseudomonas sp. was selected, as it is a well k

- [page 1] radation studies, Pseudomonas sp. was selected, as it is a well known biodegrader of agrochemicals [16–18] and its occurrence was reported earlier in tea rhizosphere [19]. 2. Materials and methods The pesticide used in the current study was reference standard propargite with purity of 95.4%, obtained from Riedel-de-Haen, Germany. The chemical was dissolved in acetone for further stud- ies. In the present study, degradation of the target compound (propargite) only was studied, not its metabolites. The detection and quantiﬁcation limit ranges for propargite in this study was 0304-3894/$ – see front matter © 2009 Published by Elsevier B.V. doi:10.1016/j.jhazmat.2009.09.050

- [page 1] REASONED OPINION APPROVED: 12 February 2018 doi: 10.2903/j.efsa.2018.5193 Setting of maximum residue limits for propargite in citrus fruits and tea European Food Safety Authority (EFSA) Abstract In accordance with Article 6 of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005, the evaluating Member State (EMS) Italy received an application from Arysta LifeScience Great Britain Ltd to set maximum residue levels (MRL) for the active substance propargite in imported citrus fruits and tea. Italy drafted an evaluation report in accordance with Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005, which was submitted to the European Commission and forwarded to EFSA. Accordi

- [page 1] afety Authority (EFSA) Abstract In accordance with Article 6 of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005, the evaluating Member State (EMS) Italy received an application from Arysta LifeScience Great Britain Ltd to set maximum residue levels (MRL) for the active substance propargite in imported citrus fruits and tea. Italy drafted an evaluation report in accordance with Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005, which was submitted to the European Commission and forwarded to EFSA. According to EFSA, the data are suf ﬁcient to derive a MRL proposal of 4 mg/kg for oranges only, as the extrapolation to the whole group is not supported by the EU rules. Based on the residue trials, an MRL of 50 mg/kg for tea could be derived. However , a risk manager decision is required whether the setting of a MR

- [page 1] erived. However , a risk manager decision is required whether the setting of a MRL of 50 mg/kg for tea is acceptable since the MRL reported to be into force in India is 10 mg/kg. Adequate analytical enforcement methods are available to control the residues of propargite on the commodities under consideration. Since the enforcement method of analysis is not enantioselective, residues are determined as the sum of any possible isomer ratio of the active substance propargite. EFSA concluded that the use of propargite on oranges and tea as reported in the countries of origin will not result in a consumer exposure exceeding the toxicological reference values and therefore is unlikely to pose a consumer health risk. However , it should be noted that the risk assessment is affected by un

- [page 1] ement methods are available to control the residues of propargite on the commodities under consideration. EFSA concluded that the use of propargite on oranges and tea as reported in the countries of origin will not result in a consumer exposure exceeding the toxicological reference values and therefore is unlikely to pose a consumer health risk. However , it should be noted that the risk assessment is affected by uncertainties linked to the toxicological pro ﬁle and the reliability of results for some of the metabolites included in the residue de ﬁnition and it is applicable to the technical propargite with the isome

- [page 3] Summary In accordance with Article 6 of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005, the evaluating Member State (EMS) Italy received an application from Arysta LifeScience Great Britain Ltd to set maximum residue levels (MRLs) for the active substance propargite in citrus fruits and tea. Italy proposed the MRL of 4 mg/kg for oranges imported from Brazil, as the extrapolation to the whole group is not supported, and of 50 mg/kg for tea imported from India. Italy drafted an evaluation report in accordance with Article 8 of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005, which was submitted to the European Commission and forwarded to the European Food Safety Authority (EFSA). EFSA based its assessment on the updated evaluation report submitted by the EMS, the draft assessment report and the ad

- [page 3] Safety Authority (EFSA). EFSA based its assessment on the updated evaluation report submitted by the EMS, the draft assessment report and the additional report to the draft assessment report prepared under Directive 91/414/EEC, the Commission review report on propargite, the conclusion on the peer review of the pesticide risk assessment of the active substance propargite, the Joint Meeting on Pesticide Residues (JMPR) Evaluation report as well as the conclusion from the previous EFSA opinion on the review of the existing MRLs according to Article 12 of Regulation (EC) No 396/2005 for propargite. The toxicological pro ﬁle of propargite was assessed in the framework of the European Union (EU) pesticides peer review. Additional data were submitted with this import tolerance request
